The **National Rally** ([French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language): *Rassemblement National* [\[ʁasɑ̃bləmɑ̃ nɑsjɔnal\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/French), **RN**), known as the **National Front** from 1972 to 2018 (French: *Front national* [\[fʁɔ̃ nɑsjɔnal\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/French), **FN**), is a French [far-right](221220.md) political party, described as [right-wing populist](8354382.md) and [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nationalism). It has been the single largest [parliamentary opposition party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally_group_\(National_Assembly\)) in the [National Assembly](299616.md) since 2022. It [opposes immigration](6491626.md), advocating significant cuts to [legal immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_immigration), protection of French identity,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies201246–55-13] and stricter control of [illegal immigration](7124412.md).

The party was founded in 1972 by the [Ordre Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordre_Nouveau_\(1960s\)), and notably by [Jean-Marie Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Le_Pen); former [Nazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) ([Waffen-SS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waffen-SS)) members [Pierre Bousquet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Bousquet) and [Léon Gaultier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Gaultier); [neo-Nazi](54361.md) sympathizers such as [François Duprat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Duprat); and supporters nostalgic for [French Algeria](292341.md), such as [Roger Holeindre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Holeindre), a member of the "[Organisation armée secrète](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_arm%C3%A9e_secr%C3%A8te)".[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16] Ordre Nouveau sought to establish the party as a legitimate political vehicle for the far-right movement.[^cite_note-17] Jean-Marie Le Pen was its leader until his resignation in 2011. While its influence was marginal until 1984, the party's role as a nationalist electoral force has grown considerably.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007229-18] It has put forward a candidate at every presidential election but one since 1974. In the [2002 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_French_presidential_election), Jean-Marie Le Pen advanced to the second round but finished a distant second in the runoff to [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199946,_56_and_71-19] His daughter [Marine Le Pen](572897.md) was elected to succeed him as party leader in 2012. [Jordan Bardella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Bardella) assumed the leadership in 2022.[^cite_note-\:2-20]

The party has seen an increase in its popularity and acceptance in French society in recent years. It has been accused of promoting [xenophobia](34141.md) and [antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_France).[^cite_note-\:1-21] While her father was nicknamed the "Devil of the Republic" by mainstream media and sparked outrage for [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech), including [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) and [Islamophobia](162173.md), Marine Le Pen pursued a policy of "[de-demonisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9diabolisation)", trying to frame the party as being neither right nor left.[^cite_note-22] She endeavoured to extract it from its far-right roots, as well as censuring controversial members like her father, who was suspended and then expelled from the party in 2015.[^cite_note-23] Following her election as the leader of the party in 2011, the popularity of the FN grew.[^cite_note-24] By 2015, the FN had established itself as a major political party in France.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26] Sources traditionally label the party as far-right,[^cite_note-National_Rally_far-right-8] though some outlets argue it has substantially moderated from its years under Le Pen to be classified as "right-wing populist" or "nationalist right".[^cite_note-\:6-27]

On 31 March 2025, 25 National Rally members (including Marine Le Pen, former MEPs, and their assistants) were [convicted of embezzlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_assistants_affair) for using [European Parliament](9581.md) funds to fund National Front staff from 2004 to 2016. The sentences for several MEPs, including Le Pen, included bans on running for political office.[^cite_note-Gdn31325-28][^cite_note-TelegraphConviction-29][^cite_note-France24Conviction-30] Le Pen's appeal with a Paris appeals court was heard in early 2026, which ruled that while she was guilty, her sentence would be shortened, enabling her to run in the [2027 French Presidential Election](72162761.md).[^cite_note-31]

## Party name

At the FN congress of 2018, Marine Le Pen proposed renaming the party *Rassemblement National* (National Rally),[^cite_note-Le_Monde,_March_2018-32] and this was confirmed by a ballot of party members.[^cite_note-Le_Monde,_June_2018-33] Formerly strongly [Eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurosceptic), the National Rally changed policies in 2019, deciding to campaign for a reform of the EU rather than leaving it and to keep the [euro](9472.md) as the main currency of France (together with the [CFP franc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFP_franc) for some collectivities).[^cite_note-34] In 2021, Le Pen announced that she wanted to remain in the [Schengen Area](16565139.md), but to reserve free movement to nationals of a [European Economic Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Area) country, excluding residents of and visitors from another Schengen country.[^cite_note-\:3-35][^cite_note-36]

## History

### Background

Main article: [Ordre Nouveau (1960s)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordre_Nouveau_\(1960s\))

The party's ideological roots can be traced to both [Poujadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poujadism), a [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populist), small business [tax protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_protest) movement founded in 1953 by [Pierre Poujade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Poujade) and on right-wing dismay over the decision by French President [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle) to abandon his promise of holding on to the [colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony) of [French Algeria](292341.md), (many *frontistes*, including Le Pen, were part of an inner circle of returned servicemen known as *Le cercle national des combattants*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies201231–35-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199921–24-38] During the [1965 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_French_presidential_election), Le Pen unsuccessfully attempted to consolidate the right-wing vote around presidential candidate [Jean-Louis Tixier-Vignancour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Louis_Tixier-Vignancour).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199925–27-39] Throughout the late 1960s and early 1970s, the French far-right consisted mainly of small, extreme movements such as *[Occident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occident_\(movement\))*, *[Groupe Union Défense](320279.md)* (GUD), and the *[Ordre Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordre_Nouveau_\(1960s\))* (ON).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199927–31-40]

Espousing France's Catholic and monarchist traditions, one of the primary progenitors of the ideology generally promoted by FN was the *[Action Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Fran%C3%A7aise)*, founded at the end of the 19th century, and its descendants in the *Restauration Nationale*, a pro-[monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy) group that supports the claim of the [Count of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Count_of_Paris) to the French throne.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199913–17-41][^cite_note-42]

### Early years

#### Foundation (1972–1973)

While *Ordre Nouveau* had competed in some local elections since 1970, at its second congress, in June 1972, it decided to establish a new political party to contest the [1973 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_French_legislative_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007163–164-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199936_f-44] The party was launched on 5 October 1972 under the name **National Front for French Unity** (*Front national pour l'unité française*), or **Front National**.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007169-45] In order to create a broad movement, ON sought to model the new party on the more established [Italian Social Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Social_Movement) (MSI), which at the time appeared to establish a broad coalition of the Italian hard right. The FN adopted a French version of the MSI tricolour flame as its logo.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007159,_169-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199931,_36–37-47][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitscheltMcGann199794-48] ON wanted to unite the various French far-right currents, and brought together "nationals" of Le Pen's group and [Roger Holeindre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Holeindre)'s Party of French Unity; "nationalists" from [Pierre Bousquet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Bousquet)'s *Militant* movement or [François Brigneau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Brigneau)'s and Alain Robert's [Ordre Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordre_Nouveau_\(1960s\)); the [anti-Gaullist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Gaullist) [Georges Bidault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Bidault)'s Justice and Liberty movement; as well as former [Poujadists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poujadists), Algerian War veterans, and some monarchists, among others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007169-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199913-49][^cite_note-50] Le Pen was chosen to be the first president of the party, as he was untainted with the militant public image of the ON and was a relatively moderate figure in the far-right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199938_f-51][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007170-52]

The National Front fared poorly in the [1973 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_French_legislative_election), receiving 0.5% of the national vote, although Le Pen won 5% in his Paris constituency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007171-53] In 1973, the party created a youth movement, the *Front national de la jeunesse* (National Front of Youth; FNJ). The rhetoric used in the campaign stressed old, far-right themes and was largely uninspiring to the electorate at the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199939-54] Otherwise, its official program at this point was relatively moderate, differing little from the mainstream right's.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007173_f-55] Le Pen sought the "total fusion" of the currents in the party, and warned against "crude activism."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007174_f-56] The FNJ were banned from the party later that year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007175-57][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199939-54] The move towards the mainstream cost it many leading members and much of its militant base.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007175-57]

In the [1974 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_French_presidential_election), Le Pen failed to find a mobilising theme for his campaign,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007176_f-58] since many of its platform's major issues, such as [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism), were shared by most of the mainstream right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007183-59] Other FN issues included calls for increased French birth rates, immigration reduction (although this was downplayed), establishment of a professional army, abrogation of the [Évian Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89vian_Accords), and generally the creation of a "French and European [renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance)."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007177,_185-60] Despite being the only nationalist candidate, he failed to gain the support of the whole of the far-right, as the various groups either rallied behind other candidates or called for voter abstention.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007177-61] The campaign further lost ground when the [Revolutionary Communist League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Communist_League_\(France\)) made public a report of Le Pen's alleged involvement in torture during his time in Algeria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007177-61] In his first participation in a presidential election, Le Pen won only 0.8% of the national vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007177-61]

#### FN–PFN rivalry (1973–1981)

Following the 1974 election, the FN was obscured by the appearance of the [Party of New Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_New_Forces) (PFN), founded by FN dissidents (largely from the ON).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199941-62][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007178_f-63] Their competition weakened both parties throughout the 1970s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199941-62] Along with the growing influence of [François Duprat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Duprat) and his "[revolutionary nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Nationalist_Groups)", the FN gained several new groups of supporters in the late 1970s and early 1980s: [Jean-Pierre Stirbois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Stirbois) (1977) and his "[solidarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporatism#Corporate_solidarism)", [Bruno Gollnisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Gollnisch) (1983), [Bernard Antony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Antony) (1984) and his Catholic fundamentalists, as well as [Jean-Yves Le Gallou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Le_Gallou) (1985) and the [Nouvelle Droite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nouvelle_Droite).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007180–184-64][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg2017121-65] Following the death of Duprat in a bomb attack in 1978, the revolutionary nationalists left the party, while Stirbois became Le Pen's deputy as his solidarists effectively ousted the [neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism) tendency in the party leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007181,_184-66] A radical group split off in 1980 and founded the [French Nationalist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Nationalist_Party), dismissing the FN as becoming "too [Zionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism)" with Le Pen being a "puppet of the Jews."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamusLebourg2017106-67] The far right was marginalised altogether in the [1978 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_French_legislative_election), although the PFN came out better off.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007179–180,_185–187-68][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199943-69] In the first election for the [European Parliament in 1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_European_Parliament_election_in_France), the PFN became part of an attempt to build a "Euro-Right" alliance of the continent's far-right parties, and was in the end the only one of the two that contested the election.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007181_f-70] It fielded Jean-Louis Tixier-Vignancour as its primary candidate, while Le Pen called for voter abstention.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007182-71]

For the [1981 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_French_presidential_election), both Le Pen and Pascal Gauchon of the PFN declared their intentions to run.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007182-71] However, an increased requirement regarding obtaining signatures of support from elected officials had been introduced for the election, which left both Le Pen and Gauchon unable to participate.[^cite_note-72]

The election was won by [François Mitterrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mitterrand) of the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_\(France\)) (PS), a results that brought the [political left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_left) to national power for the first time in the Fifth Republic; Mitterrand immediately dissolved the National Assembly and called a snap legislative election.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007182,_198-73] With only three weeks to prepare its campaign, the FN fielded only a limited number of candidates and won only 0.2% of the national vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007183-59] The PFN was even worse off, and the election marked the effective end of competition from the party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007183-59] The Socialists attained their best ever result with an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_majority) in the [1981 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_French_legislative_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007182_f-74]

The "socialist takeover" led to a radicalisation in centre-right, anti-communist, and anti-socialist voters.[^cite_note-75]

### Jean-Marie Le Pen's leadership

#### Electoral breakthrough (1982–1988)



[Jean-Marie Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Le_Pen), leader of the National Front from 1972 to 2011

While the French party system had been dominated by polarisation and competition between the clear-cut ideological alternatives of two political blocs in the 1970s, the two blocs had largely moved towards the centre by the mid-1980s. This led many voters to perceive the blocs as more or less indistinguishable, particularly after the [Socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_\(France\))' "austerity turn" (*tournant de la rigueur*) of 1983,[^cite_note-76] in turn inducing them to seek out to new political alternatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitscheltMcGann199795–98-77] By October 1982, Le Pen supported the prospect of deals with the mainstream right, provided that the FN did not have to soften its position on "key issues."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007195-78] In the [1983 municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_French_municipal_elections), the centre-right [Rally for the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rally_for_the_Republic) (RPR) and the centrist [Union for French Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_French_Democracy) (UDF) formed alliances with the FN in a number of towns.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007195-78] The most notable result came in the [20th arrondissement of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_arrondissement_of_Paris), where Le Pen was elected to the local council with 11% of the vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007195-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199960-79] Subsequent by-elections kept media attention on the party, which was for the first time able to pose as a viable component of the broader right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007196-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199961-81] In a by-election in [Dreux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreux) in October 1983, the FN won 17% of the vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007195-78] With the choice of defeat to the political left or dealing with the FN, the local RPR and UDF agreed to form an alliance with the FN, causing a national sensation;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007195-78] together, they won the second round with 55% of the vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199960-79] The events in Dreux were a monumental turning point in the rise of the FN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitscheltMcGann1997100-82]

Le Pen protested the "media boycott" against his party by sending letters to President Mitterrand in mid-1982.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007196-80] Following an exchange of letters with Le Pen, Mitterrand instructed the heads of the main television channels to give equitable coverage to the FN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007196-80] In January 1984, the party made its first appearance in a monthly poll of political popularity, in which 9% of respondents held a "positive opinion" of the FN and some support for Le Pen personally.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007196-80] The next month, Le Pen was, for the first time, invited on a prime-time television interview programme, which he himself later deemed "the hour that changed everything".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007196-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199976-83]

In the June [1984 European elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_European_Parliament_election_in_France), the FN won 11% of the vote and ten seats,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199962-84][^cite_note-85] in a contest that was considered to have a low level of importance by the public, which played to the party's advantage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199963-86] The FN, notably, made inroads in both right-wing and left-wing constituencies, and finished 2nd in a number of towns.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007194-87] While many Socialists had arguably exploited the party in order to divide the right,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007230-88] Mitterrand later conceded that he had underestimated Le Pen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007196-80] By July, 17% of opinion poll respondents held a positive opinion of the FN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007197-89]

By the early 1980s, the FN featured a mosaic of ideological tendencies and attracted figures who were previously resistant to the party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007197-89] The party managed to draw supporters from the mainstream right, including some high-profile defectors from the RPR, the UDF, and the [National Centre of Independents and Peasants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Centre_of_Independents_and_Peasants) (CNIP).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007197-89] In the 1984 European elections, eleven of the 81 FN candidates came from these parties, while the party's list also included an [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_France) and a [Jew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_France) - although in unwinnable positions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007197-89] Former [collaborators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collaboration_with_Nazi_Germany_and_Fascist_Italy#France) were also accepted in the party, as Le Pen urged the need for "reconciliation", arguing that forty years after the war the only important question was whether or not "they wish to serve their country".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007197-89] The FN won 8.7% overall support in the [1985 cantonal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_French_cantonal_elections), netting over 30% in some areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007209-90]

For the [1986 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_French_legislative_election), the FN took advantage of the new, proportional representation system[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199966-92] and won 9.8% of the vote and 35 seats in the National Assembly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007209-90] Many of these seats were filled by a new wave of "respectable" political operatives, *notables*, who had joined the party after its 1984 success.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199964–66-93][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007216-94] The RPR won a majority with smaller, centre-right parties, and thus avoided the need to deal with the FN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007209-90] Although FN was unable to exercise any real political influence, the party could project an image of political legitimacy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007216-94][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199980-95] Several of its legislative proposals were controversial and had a socially reactionary and xenophobic character, among them attempts to restore the [death penalty](5902.md), expel foreigners who "proportionally committed more crimes than the French", restrict naturalisation, introduce a "national preference" for employment, impose taxes on the hiring of foreigners by French companies, and privatise [Agence France-Presse](471981.md).[^cite_note-96]

The party's time in the National Assembly effectively came to an end when [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac) reinstated the two-round system of majority voting for the next election.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007217-97] In [the regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_French_regional_elections) held on the same day, FN won 137 seats, and gained representation in 21 of the 22 French regional councils.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007209-90] The RPR depended on FN support to win presidencies in some regional councils, and the FN won vice-presidential posts in four regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007209-90]

#### Consolidation (1988–1997)

Le Pen's campaign for the presidential election unofficially began in the months following the 1986 election.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007219-98] To promote his statesmanship credentials, he made trips to South East Asia, the United States, and Africa.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007219-98] The management of the formal campaign, launched in April 1987, was entrusted to [Bruno Mégret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_M%C3%A9gret), one of the new *notables*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007219-98] With his entourage, Le Pen traversed France for the entire period and, helped by Mégret, employed an American-style campaign.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199968-99] Le Pen's presidential campaign was highly successful; no candidates came close to rival his ability to excite audiences at rallies and boost ratings at television appearances.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007219-98] Using a [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) tone, he presented himself as the representative of the people against the "gang of four" (RPR, UDF, PS, [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_France)), while the central theme of his campaign was "national preference".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007219-98] In the [1988 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_French_presidential_election), Le Pen won an unprecedented 14.4% of the vote,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007224-100] and double the votes of 1984.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199970-101]

In the snap [1988 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_French_legislative_election), the FN was hurt by the return of two-ballot majority voting, by the limited campaign period, and by the departure of many *notables*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199980-95][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007227-102] In the election, the party retained its 9.8% support from the previous legislative election, but was reduced to a single seat in the National Assembly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007227-102] Following some [anti-Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Semitism) comments made by Le Pen and the FN newspaper *National Hebdo* in the late 1980s, some valuable FN politicians left the party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007223_f-103][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199989-104] Soon, other quarrels left the party without its remaining member of the National Assembly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199990-105] In November 1988, general secretary Jean-Pierre Stirbois, who, together with his wife Marie-France, had been instrumental in the FN's early electoral successes, died in a car accident, leaving Bruno Mégret as the unrivalled, de facto FN deputy leader.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007219-98][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199990-105] The party only got 5% in the [1988 cantonal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_French_cantonal_elections), while the RPR announced it would reject any alliance with the FN, a rejections that now included the local level.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007233-106]

In the [1989 European elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_European_Parliament_election_in_France), the FN held on to its ten seats, winning 11.7% of the vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007234-107]

In the wake of FN's electoral success, the immigration debate, growing concerns over Islamic fundamentalism, and [the *fatwa*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Satanic_Verses_controversy) against [Salman Rushdie](28722.md) by [Ayatollah Khomeini](26232.md), the 1989 *[affaire du foulard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_debate_over_veils)* was the first major test of the relations between the values of the French Republic and [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007235–237-108] Following that success, surveys found that French public opinion was largely negative towards Islam.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007237-109] In a 1989 legislative by-election in Dreux, FN candidate Marie-France Stirbois, campaigning mostly on an anti-[Islamism](15012.md) platform, returned a symbolic FN presence to the National Assembly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007236_f-110] By the early 1990s, some mainstream politicians began also employing anti-immigration rhetoric.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199993-111] In the first round of the [1993 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_French_legislative_election), the FN soared to 12.7% of the overall vote, but did not win a single seat due to the nature of the electoral system.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007247–249-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair199994_f-114] In the [1995 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_French_presidential_election), votes for Le Pen rose to 15% of the total.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007252-115]

The FN won an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_majority) (and thus the mayorship) in three cities in the [1995 municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_French_municipal_elections): [Toulon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toulon), [Marignane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marignane), and [Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange,_Vaucluse).[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007260_f-118] Le Pen then declared that his party would implement its "national preference" policy, with the risk of provoking the central government and being at odds with the laws of the Republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007261-116] The FN's elected representatives pursued interventionist policies with regards to the new cultural complexion of their towns by directly influencing artistic events, cinema schedules, and library holdings, as well as cutting or halting subsidies for multicultural associations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007262_f-119] The party won [Vitrolles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitrolles,_Bouches-du-Rh%C3%B4ne), its fourth town, in a 1997 by-election, where similar policies were subsequently pursued.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007263-120] Vitrolles' new mayor [Catherine Mégret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_M%C3%A9gret?action=edit\&redlink=1), who ran in place of her husband Bruno,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair1999101-121] went further in one significant measure, introducing a special 5,000-[franc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_franc) allowance for babies born to at least one parent of French (or EU) nationality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007263-120] The measure was ruled illegal by a court, which sentenced her to a suspended prison sentence, a fine, and a two-year ban from office.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007263-120]

#### Turmoil and split of the MNR (1997–2002)



[Bruno Mégret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_M%C3%A9gret) and his faction broke out from the FN to form the MNR party.

In the [1997 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_French_legislative_election), the FN polled its best-ever result with 15.3% support in [metropolitan France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_France).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007264-122][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair1999104-123] The result showed that the party had become established enough to compete without its leader, who had decided not to run, in order to focus on the 2002 presidential election.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeClair1999103-124] Although it won only one seat in the National Assembly, in [Toulon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toulon),[^cite_note-125] it advanced to the second round in 132 constituencies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007264_f-126] The FN was arguably more influential at that time than it had been in 1986 with its 35 seats.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007275-127] While Bruno Mégret and [Bruno Gollnisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Gollnisch), favoured tactical cooperation with a weakened centre-right following the left's victory, Le Pen rejected any such "compromise."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007276-128] In the tenth FN national congress in 1997, Mégret stepped up his position in the party as its rising star and a potential leader following Le Pen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007271_f-129] Le Pen however refused to designate Mégret as his successor-elect, and instead made his wife Jany the leader of the FN list for the upcoming European election.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007277–279-130]

Mégret and his faction left the FN in January 1999 and founded the [National Republican Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Republican_Movement) (MNR), effectively splitting the FN in half at most levels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007279-131][^cite_note-oxfn-132] Many of those who joined the new MNR had joined the FN in the mid-1980s, in part from the *Nouvelle Droite*, with a vision of building bridges to the parliamentary right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007279-131] Many had also been particularly influential in intellectualising the FN's policies on immigration, identity, and "national preference". Following the split, Le Pen denounced them as "extremist" and "racist".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007279-131] Support for the two parties was almost equal in the [1999 European election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_European_Parliament_election_in_France), as the FN polled its lowest national score since 1984 with just 5.7%, and the MNR won 3.3%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007280-133] The effects of the split, and competition from more moderate nationalists, resulted in their combined support being lower than the FN result of 1984.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007280_f-134]

#### Presidential run-off (2002)



Logo for Le Pen's 2002 presidential campaign

For the [2002 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_French_presidential_election), opinion polls had predicted a run-off between incumbent President Chirac and Socialist candidate [Lionel Jospin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lionel_Jospin).[^cite_note-tel1-135][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007281-136] In a shock outcome, Le Pen outperformed Jospin (by 0.7%) in the 1st round, placing second and advancing to the runoff.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007281-136] This resulted in the first presidential run-off since 1969 without a leftist candidate and the first ever with a candidate from the far-right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007282-137] To Le Pen's advantage, the election campaign had increasingly focused on law-and-order issues, helped by media attention on a number of violent incidents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007283-138] Jospin had also been weakened due to the competition between an exceptional number of leftist parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007284-139] Nevertheless, Chirac did not even have to campaign in the second round, as widespread anti-Le Pen protests from the media and public opinion culminated on [May Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Day) in a demonstration of 1.5 million participants across France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007288_f-140] Chirac also refused to debate with Le Pen, and the traditional televised debate was cancelled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007289-141] In the end, Chirac won the presidential run-off with an unprecedented 82.2% of the vote, with 71% of his votes—according to polls—cast simply "to block Le Pen".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007289-141] Following the presidential election, the main centre-right parties merged to form the broad-based [Union for a Popular Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_a_Popular_Movement) (UMP).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007291-142] The FN failed to hold on to Le Pen's support for the [2002 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_French_legislative_election), in which it got 11.3% of the vote.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007291–293-143] It nevertheless outpolled Mégret's MNR, which had fielded the same number of candidates but won a mere 1.1% support.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007292_f-144]

#### Decline (2003–2010)



National advertisement in [Marseille](40888948.md) for Le Pen's 2007 presidential bid

A new electoral system of two-round voting had been introduced for the [2004 regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_French_regional_elections), in part in an attempt to reduce the FN's influence in regional councils.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007297-145] The FN won 15.1% of the vote in metropolitan France, almost the same as in 1998, but its number of councillors was almost halved due to the new system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007298-146] For the [2004 European elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_European_Parliament_election_in_France), too, a new system less favourable to the FN had been introduced.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007300-147] The party regained some of its strength from 1999, earning 9.8% of the vote and seven seats.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2007300-147]

For the [2007 presidential election](11105698.md), Le Pen and Mégret agreed to join forces. Le Pen came 4th in the election with 11% of the vote, and the party won no seats in the [legislative election of the same year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_French_legislative_election). The party's 4.3% support was the lowest figure since the 1981 election and only one candidate, [Marine Le Pen](572897.md) in [Pas de Calais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pas_de_Calais), reached the runoff -where she was defeated by the Socialist incumbent. These electoral defeats partly accounted for the party's financial problems. Le Pen announced the sale of the FN headquarters in [Saint-Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Cloud), *Le Paquebot*, and of his personal armoured car.[^cite_note-148] In 2008, a French court handed Le Pen a three-month suspended sentence and a €10,000 fine for remarks he made in 2005 that contravened France's law against [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial).[^cite_note-149] Twenty permanent employees of the FN were also dismissed in 2008.[^cite_note-150]

In the [2010 regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_French_regional_elections) the FN appeared to have re-emerged on the political scene after surprisingly winning almost 12% of the overall vote and 118 seats.[^cite_note-151]

### Marine Le Pen's leadership

#### Revival of the FN (2011–2012)

Main article: [2012 Marine Le Pen presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Marine_Le_Pen_presidential_campaign)



[Marine Le Pen](572897.md), National Front president (2011–2022)



Results by region at the first round of the [2015 French regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_French_regional_elections), with regions where the National Front gained the most votes in grey

Jean-Marie Le Pen announced in September 2008 that he would retire as FN president in 2010.[^cite_note-tel1-135] Le Pen's daughter Marine Le Pen and FN executive vice-president Bruno Gollnisch campaigned to succeed Le Pen,[^cite_note-tel1-135] with Marine's candidacy backed by her father.[^cite_note-tel1-135] On 15 January 2011, it was announced that Marine Le Pen had received the two-thirds vote needed to become the new leader of the FN.[^cite_note-bbc1-152][^cite_note-bbc2-153] She embarked on a project to transform the FN into a "mainstream party" by softening its xenophobic image.[^cite_note-tel1-135][^cite_note-bbc1-152][^cite_note-bbc2-153] Opinion polls showed the party's popularity increase under Marine Le Pen, and in the [2011 cantonal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_French_cantonal_elections) the party won 15% of the overall vote (up from 4.5% in 2008). However, due to the French electoral system, the party only won 2 of the 2,026 seats that were up for election.[^cite_note-154] From January 2011 to December 2014 the FN's membership roughly tripled.[^cite_note-155]

At the end of 2011, the National Front withdrew from the far-right [Alliance of European National Movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_of_European_National_Movements) and joined the more moderate [European Alliance of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Alliance_for_Freedom). In October 2013, Bruno Gollnisch and Jean-Marie Le Pen resigned from their position in the AENM.

For the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_French_presidential_election), opinion polls showed Marine Le Pen as a serious challenger, with a few polls even suggesting that she could win the first round of the election.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] In the event, Le Pen came 3rd in the first round, scoring 17.9% – the best showing ever in a presidential election for the FN at that time.

In the [2012 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_French_legislative_election), the National Front won two seats: [Gilbert Collard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilbert_Collard) and [Marion Maréchal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_Mar%C3%A9chal).[^cite_note-DT2MMLP-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160]

In two polls about presidential favourites, conducted in April and May 2013,[^cite_note-nouvelobs1-161] Marine le Pen polled ahead of president [François Hollande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande) but behind [Nicolas Sarkozy](557007.md).[^cite_note-nouvelobs1-161]

#### Electoral successes (2012–2017)



Demonstration against the National Front in Paris after the results of the 2014 election

&#x20;In the [municipal elections held on 23 and 30 March 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_French_municipal_elections), lists officially supported by the National Front won mayoralties in 12 cities: [Beaucaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaucaire,_Gard), [Cogolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cogolin), [Fréjus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9jus), [Hayange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayange), [Hénin-Beaumont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A9nin-Beaumont), [Le Luc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Luc), [Le Pontet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Pontet,_Vaucluse), [Mantes-la-Ville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantes-la-Ville), [the 7th arrondissement of Marseille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Marseille), [Villers-Cotterêts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villers-Cotter%C3%AAts), [Béziers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A9ziers) and [Camaret-sur-Aigues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camaret-sur-Aigues). While some of these cities were in southern France (like Fréjus) which traditionally votes more for right-wing parties than the rest of the country, others were located in northern France, where Socialist Party had been strong until the 2010s. Following these elections, the National Front had, in cities of over 1,000 inhabitants, 1,546 and 459 councilors at two different levels of local government.[^cite_note-162] The international media described the results as "historic".[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

The National Front received 4,712,461 votes in the [2014 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_France), finishing first with 24.86% of the vote and 24 of France's 74 seats.[^cite_note-166] This was said to be "the first time the anti-immigrant, anti-EU party had won a nationwide election in its four-decade history."[^cite_note-167] The party's success came as a "shock" in France and the EU.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]

#### Front National becomes Rassemblement National (2018)

At the conclusion of the 11 March 2018 party congress in Lille, Marine Le Pen proposed renaming the party to *Rassemblement national* (National Rally) while keeping the flame as its logo.[^cite_note-Le_Monde,_March_2018-32] The name was not unknown in French politics: it recalled both the [National Popular Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Popular_Rally), a collaborationist party founded by [Marcel Déat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_D%C3%A9at) in 1941 with the help of [Eugène Deloncle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_Deloncle), founder of [La Cagoule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Cagoule), and led by [Georges Albertini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Albertini?action=edit\&redlink=1) during the Occupation[^cite_note-170] and the [Rassemblement National Français](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rassemblement_National_Fran%C3%A7ais), founded by the far-right lawyer and politician [Jean-Louis Tixier-Vignancour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Louis_Tixier-Vignancour), whose [presidential campaign in 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_French_presidential_election) was managed by Jean-Marie Le Pen.[^cite_note-171] It had already been used by the FN for [its parliamentary group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groupe_Front_national_%E2%80%93_Rassemblement_national?action=edit\&redlink=1) between 1986 and 1988. Nevertheless, the name change faced opposition from an already-existing party named "Rassemblement national", whose president, Igor Kurek, described the group as "Gaullist and republican right" and who had registered the name with the [INPI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_of_Industrial_Property_\(France\)) in 2013.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] On 1 June, Le Pen announced that the name change was approved by party adherents with 80.81% in favour.[^cite_note-Le_Monde,_June_2018-33]

#### Presidential and parliamentary election, rebranding (2017–2022)

Main article: [2017 Marine Le Pen presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Marine_Le_Pen_presidential_campaign)

On 24 April 2017, a day after the first round of the [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election), Marine Le Pen announced that she would temporarily step down as the party's leader in an attempt to "unite voters."[^cite_note-auto-174] In the second round of voting, Le Pen was defeated 66.1% to 33.9% by her rival [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) of [En Marche!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/En_Marche!)[^cite_note-175]

During the [following parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_legislative_election), the FN received 13.02% of the vote, a little lower than the 13.07% of the 2012 elections. The party appeared to have suffered from a demobilisation of its voters from the previous vote. Nonetheless, eight deputies (six FN and two affiliated) were elected, the best number for the FN in a parliamentary election using a majoritarian electoral system since its creation.[^cite_note-176] Marine Le Pen was elected to the National Assembly for the first time, while [Gilbert Collard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilbert_Collard) was re-elected. FN's 23-year-old [Ludovic Pajot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludovic_Pajot) became the youngest ever member of the French parliament.

In late 2017, [Florian Philippot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florian_Philippot) left the FN and formed [The Patriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Patriots_\(France\)), on the grounds that the FN had "softened" its position on leaving the [EU](9317.md) and abandoning the [Euro](9472.md).[^cite_note-177]

In 2018, [Steve Bannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Bannon), former advisor to Donald Trump before and after his 2016 election, gave what has been described as a "populist [pep talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pep_talk)".[^cite_note-Nossiter-178] Bannon advised the party members to "Let them call you racist, let them call you xenophobes, let them call you nativists. Wear it like a badge of honor. Because every day, we get stronger and they get weaker. ... History is on our side and will bring us victory." Bannon's remarks brought the members to their feet.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181]

In January 2019, ex-Sarkozy minister [Thierry Mariani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thierry_Mariani) and former conservative lawmaker [Jean-Paul Garraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Paul_Garraud), left [Les Républicains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_\(France\)) (LR) and joined the National Rally.[^cite_note-182]

During a 2021 debate, Marine Le Pen was called "soft" on Islam by the Minister of the Interior in Macron's government, [Gérald Darmanin](54070416.md).[^cite_note-183] Marine Le Pen called for a "national-unity government" that would include persons such as [Nicolas Dupont-Aignan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Dupont-Aignan), former LR officials, and [souverainistes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Souverainism) on the left, such as former economy minister [Arnaud Montebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnaud_Montebourg).[^cite_note-184]

In the months before the [2021 French regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_French_regional_elections), political commentators noted an increased moderation in the FN's platform that aimed to attract conservative voters,[^cite_note-185] as well as a new image being promoted by the party as a force of *la droite populaire*, i.e. the popular right, the social right.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] The party, nonetheless, fared badly in these elections.[^cite_note-188]

In the [2022 French presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_presidential_election), Le Pen again reached the 2nd round with 23.15% of the votes, though she was defeated by incumbent Macron, after receiving 41.45% of the votes in the run-off.[^cite_note-189] Marine Le Pen received 13 million votes in the second round, more than double the 5.25 million Jean Marie Le Pen received in 2002.[^cite_note-190]

In the [2022 French legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_legislative_election), for which polling had indicated that FN would win only between 15 and 45 seats in the [National Assembly](299616.md), the party received 18.68% of the votes in the first round[^cite_note-191] and won 89 seats in the second round,[^cite_note-192] a significant increase from the previous total of 8 seats. The 89 seats enabled the National Rally to form a parliamentary group, for which at least 15 deputies are required, for the first time since 1986, when the national assembly was elected by proportional voting. The result made the party the 3rd largest party in the assembly and the largest [parliamentary opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_opposition) group.[^cite_note-193]

### Jordan Bardella's leadership

See also: [2024 The Republicans crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_The_Republicans_crisis)

[Jordan Bardella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Bardella) was elected president of the RN on 5 November 2022, ending Marine Le Pen's period as president of the party. Le Pen remained president of the RN's parliamentary group.[^cite_note-\:2-20]

#### 2024 European parliament election

In the [2024 European parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_European_Parliament_election_in_France), the RN won the most delegates of any single party (30). It won 31.4% of the vote, the first time since 1984 that a list had won more than 30% of the vote in a European Parliament election in France.[^cite_note-194] On 8 July, Bardella became president of the [Patriots for Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriots_for_Europe), originally founded by Viktor Orbán with the Czech [ANO](40808475.md) and the Austrian [FPÖ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Party_of_Austria) parties on a platform of refusing military aid to Ukraine, anti-immigration, and pro-"traditional family" values.[^cite_note-195] The group, the third largest in the parliament, also includes members from the Flemish [Vlaams Belang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Belang), the Dutch [PVV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom),[^cite_note-196] and the Italian [Lega per Salvini Premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lega_\(political_party\)).[^cite_note-197]

The National Rally's success in the European elections led to the dissolution of the French lower house by Emmanuel Macron on 9 June 2024.[^cite_note-198]

#### 2024 legislative election

Main article: [2024 French legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_French_legislative_election)

In June 2024, the party joined with the micro-party "To the right! Friends of Eric Ciotti", forming the [union of the far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_the_far-right) in the [2024 French legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_French_legislative_election).[^cite_note-Barbarit-199] Together, these parties secured the largest share of the vote in the first round with 33.15% of the vote.[^cite_note-results2024-200] More voters who reported struggling financially voted for the RN than for any other party.[^cite_note-201]

Based on their first round performance, the RN will receive around €15 million per year in government subsidies for the next five years: each vote in the first round was worth €1.61 per year to the party.[^cite_note-Barbarit-199]

According to [opinion polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_for_the_2024_French_legislative_election), the National Rally were expected to get 230–270 seats, close to an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_majority), However, 2–3 days before the second round, the RN were predicted to win around 170–220 seats. After the [exit polls](665947.md) on 7 July, that figure was reduced to 120–170 seats, and the [New Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Popular_Front) were predicted to win 150–190 seats, slightly ahead of president Macron's [Ensemble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Together_\(coalition\)) alliance. Finally, the RN won only 142 seats, ranking third in the election.

The National Rally won vast swathes of southern France, including every seat in the departement of Gard.[^cite_note-202]
